Read this first — honesty limits.

  • Maturity labels are plain-words labels from the universal research maturity scale (E1 Established, E2 Reproduced, E3 Engineering-ready, E4 Experimental, E5 Prototype, E6 Integrated, E7 Operational; T1 Theoretical, T2 Hypothetical, T3 Speculative; U Unknown). A label says how mature a technology is; it never turns a guess into a fact.
  • Any dates, horizons, or timelines here are PLANNING WINDOWS, never predictions or guaranteed breakthroughs.
  • Public records are documented evidence, never established fact. Signature records are design-study, never presented as authentic public record.
  • No consciousness or sentience claim is made here. Machine consciousness is theories and testable models only, never an established fact; no claim is made that a machine is conscious, sentient, or aware.
  • Climate restoration records are research only; no proven-intervention claims are made.
  • Post-quantum records are defensive security only.
  • Synthetic biology is design-study only, with biosafety screening; no pathogen, toxin, gene-drive, or synthesis-enabling content appears here.

Terms in Plain Words

database: a database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to. record: a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. slug: a slug is the short web-address name of a database, written with lower-case words joined by hyphens. manifest: the manifest is the master list of all databases in the network, with each database's slug, record kind, identifier prefix, and record count. record kind: a record kind is the label that says what sort of entry a record is, for example a future artificial life record. identifier prefix: an identifier prefix is the fixed letters at the start of every record identifier in one database, for example JAH-ALIFE-. generator: a generator is a program that creates new records or files on demand from a starting number called a seed, instead of storing every output in advance. seed: a seed is a starting number fed to a generator so that the same seed always produces the same output. generator gate: a generator gate is a check a generator must pass before its output is accepted, for example the emitted record kind must match the manifest record kind. JCC code: a JCC code, which stands for JAH Condensed Code, is a short code that points to one exact record, written as JAHDB followed by the database slug, a hash sign, and the record identifier. API: an API, which stands for Application Programming Interface, is a defined way for one program to ask another program for data or work. MCP: an MCP, which stands for Model Context Protocol, is a plug that lets an AI (artificial intelligence) program read a database directly. AI: AI stands for artificial intelligence, which is a program that performs tasks that usually need human thought, such as answering, classifying, or generating. SQLite: SQLite is a small database engine that stores a whole database in one file on disk. SQL: SQL, which stands for Structured Query Language, is the language used to ask questions of a relational database. JSON: JSON, which stands for JavaScript Object Notation, is a plain-text format for storing structured data with named fields. HTML: HTML, which stands for HyperText Markup Language, is the language that marks up the structure of a web page. CSS: CSS, which stands for Cascading Style Sheets, is the language that describes how a web page looks. URL: a URL, which stands for Uniform Resource Locator, is the full web address of a page or file. HTTP: HTTP, which stands for HyperText Transfer Protocol, is the set of rules a browser uses to fetch a page from a web server. HTTPS: HTTPS, which stands for HyperText Transfer Protocol Secure, is HTTP sent over a protected connection. test suite: a test suite is a collection of automated tests that check whether a program behaves as specified. demo: a demo, short for demonstration, is a small working program that runs and shows its result. website interface: a website interface is the set of web pages through which a person or a program uses a system. JAH: JAH is the initials of Justin Addam Higgins, the maker name used across the network. design-study: a design-study is a Signature-made design, an invented proposal that is labeled as a design and never presented as an established fact. UNREACHABLE: UNREACHABLE is the label recorded for a resource whose fetch failed, together with the exact reason the fetch returned. maturity label: a maturity label is a short code saying how mature a technology is, from E1 Established through E7 Operational for working technology, and T1 Theoretical through T3 Speculative for ideas not yet tested, and U for Unknown. research question: a research question is the single question a research project sets out to answer. prototype: a prototype is an early working model built to test whether an idea works. simulation: a simulation is a computer model that imitates a real system so it can be studied safely and cheaply. artificial life: artificial life is the study of life-like behaviour in systems built by people, in software, in hardware, or in laboratory chemistry, rather than life that evolved in nature. digital organism: a digital organism is a self-replicating program that lives, competes, and evolves inside a computer, like a living thing in a digital world. cellular automaton: a cellular automaton is a grid of cells that change by simple local rules, step by step, and can produce complex patterns from those rules. programmable material: a programmable material is a material whose properties, such as shape, stiffness, or colour, can be changed on command by a signal such as heat, light, electricity, or a magnetic field. fusion: fusion is the joining of light atomic nuclei to form a heavier nucleus, releasing energy, the process that powers the Sun. plasma: plasma is a hot gas of charged particles, atoms stripped of some of their electrons, that can be held by magnetic fields. tokamak: a tokamak is a doughnut-shaped chamber that holds hot plasma in place with magnetic fields so fusion can happen inside it. stellarator: a stellarator is a twisted chamber that holds hot plasma in place with magnetic fields, like a tokamak but with a more complex twisted shape. tritium: tritium is a heavy form of hydrogen, with one proton and two neutrons, used as a fusion fuel. deuterium: deuterium is a heavy form of hydrogen, with one proton and one neutron, used as a fusion fuel. Q factor: the Q factor, which is the fusion energy gain factor, is the fusion power produced divided by the heating power put in, so Q = 10 means ten times more fusion power out than heating power in. ITER: ITER, which stands for International Thermonuclear Experimental Reactor, is the large international fusion experiment being built in France. JET: JET, which stands for Joint European Torus, is the European fusion experiment in England. NIF: NIF, which stands for National Ignition Facility, is the United States laser fusion experiment in California. DOE: DOE, which stands for Department of Energy, is the United States government department for energy and science. D-T: D-T stands for deuterium-tritium, the fusion fuel pair made of the two heavy forms of hydrogen. DNA: DNA, which stands for deoxyribonucleic acid, is the molecule that carries genetic instructions in living cells. megawatt: a megawatt, shortened to MW, is one million watts, a unit of power. biosafety: biosafety is the set of practices that keep biological work safe, including screening, authorization, and human review.

What Programmable Materials Are

JAH-PROMAT-000001 · programmable materials identity · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Programmable Materials Database, catalog item 15 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is What Programmable Materials Are. A programmable material is a material whose properties, such as shape, stiffness, or colour, can be changed on command by a signal such as heat, light, electricity, or a magnetic field. An ordinary material has fixed properties: steel is stiff, rubber is soft, and neither changes unless it is cut or melted. A programmable material is designed so that a signal flips it between states: a wire that remembers a shape and returns to it when heated, a gel that swells in water and shrinks in acid, a fluid that flows freely until a magnet is switched on and then turns almost solid. The field studies four things: the trigger, which is the signal; the response, which is the change; the reversibility, which is whether the material can switch back; and the endurance, which is how many switches it survives. This record specifies the identity of the field, its four questions, and the line between a material that merely reacts and a material that is truly programmable. The system identity is fixed: this is a future programmable materials record, built as one database pair with a public edition and a Signature edition, each holding 24 records, so the pair holds 48 records in total. The public edition records the research specification as documented public knowledge and established research, with sources that were fetched and checked on 2026-11-17. The Signature edition holds Signature-made designs, each labeled design-study, which is a Signature-made design labeled as a design and never presented as an established fact. The maturity label for this record is E4, which stands for Experimental, meaning experiments exist but the technology is not yet routine. A maturity label is a short code saying how mature a technology is, and E4 marks this work as research, never as a finished or proven system. Shape-Memory Alloys

JAH-PROMAT-000002 · shape-memory alloys · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Programmable Materials Database, catalog item 15 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Shape-Memory Alloys. A shape-memory alloy is a metal that returns to a set shape when heated, and the best-known example is nitinol, an alloy of nickel and titanium. The alloy is trained by holding it in a desired shape at high temperature, then it can be bent and deformed at low temperature, and when it is heated again it snaps back to its trained shape. The cause is a change in crystal structure: at low temperature the crystals are in a soft, bendable arrangement, and at high temperature they switch to a stiff arrangement that remembers the trained shape. Shape-memory alloys are used in medical stents that unfold inside the body, in eyeglass frames that spring back after bending, and in actuators, which are parts that move things, in aircraft and robots. The research questions are how to train larger parts, how to make the switch happen at chosen temperatures, and how many cycles the alloy survives before it tires. This record specifies shape-memory alloys as documented research: their training, their crystal switch, their uses, and their limits. Shape-Memory Polymers

JAH-PROMAT-000003 · shape-memory polymers · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Programmable Materials Database, catalog item 15 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Shape-Memory Polymers. A shape-memory polymer is a plastic that returns to a set shape when triggered by heat or light, and it is the light, cheap cousin of the shape-memory alloy. The polymer is made as a network of long chains linked at points, and it is trained by warming it, stretching it into a temporary shape, and cooling it so the chains freeze in place. When it is warmed again, the chains soften and pull back to the trained shape, like a stretched rubber band returning. Polymers can be made to respond to heat, light, water, or a magnetic field, and they can hold more than one temporary shape in sequence. Researchers use them for packaging that unfolds itself, for textiles that change fit, and for medical models that expand after insertion, always as research, with no medical advice given here. The limits are strength, which is lower than metal, and speed, which is slower than an alloy snap. This record specifies shape-memory polymers as documented research: their chain network, their training, their triggers, and their limits. Electroactive Polymers

JAH-PROMAT-000004 · electroactive polymers · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Programmable Materials Database, catalog item 15 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Electroactive Polymers. An electroactive polymer is a plastic that changes shape or size under electricity, and it is the closest thing materials science has to an artificial muscle. Two families exist. One family is the dielectric elastomer, a soft film between two stretchy electrodes that squeezes thinner and spreads wider when voltage is applied, and relaxes when the voltage is removed. The other family is the ionic polymer, which bends when ions, which are charged particles, move inside it under voltage. Electroactive polymers are light, quiet, and soft, which makes them attractive for robot muscles, for pumps with no moving metal parts, and for lenses that change focus by changing shape. The research questions are how much force they can produce, how fast they can switch, and how many cycles they survive before wearing out. This record specifies electroactive polymers as documented research: their two families, their motion, their uses, and their limits. The system identity is fixed: this is a future programmable materials record, built as one database pair with a public edition and a Signature edition, each holding 24 records, so the pair holds 48 records in total. Magnetorheological Fluids

JAH-PROMAT-000005 · magnetorheological fluids · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Programmable Materials Database, catalog item 15 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Magnetorheological Fluids. A magnetorheological fluid is a liquid that thickens into a near-solid under a magnetic field, and thins back into a liquid when the field is switched off. The fluid is oil carrying tiny iron particles, and with no field the particles drift freely and the fluid pours. When a magnetic field is switched on, the particles line up into chains that resist flow, and the fluid can hold a load like a solid, and the change happens in thousandths of a second. The effect is used in car suspensions that stiffen when the road gets rough, in brakes that grip without wearing parts, and in prosthetic knees that adapt to the wearer's stride, as research and engineering, with no medical advice given here. The research questions are how to keep the particles from settling, how strong the field must be, and how the fluid behaves after millions of switches. This record specifies magnetorheological fluids as documented research: their particles, their chains, their uses, and their limits. Phase-Change Materials

JAH-PROMAT-000006 · phase-change materials · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Programmable Materials Database, catalog item 15 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Phase-Change Materials. A phase-change material is a material that stores and releases heat as it melts and freezes, and it is the simplest programmable thermal battery. When the material melts it absorbs heat without getting hotter, and when it freezes it releases that heat back, so a wall filled with it stays near its melting temperature for hours while the outside warms and cools. Common examples are waxes and salt hydrates, which are salts holding water in their crystals, chosen so their melting point sits near room temperature. The research uses them in buildings that stay cool without air conditioning, in shipping boxes that keep medicine cold, and in electronics that must not overheat, always as engineering research. The questions are how to stop the material leaking, how to keep it working after thousands of melts, and how to make it conduct heat fast enough to be useful. This record specifies phase-change materials as documented research: their melting, their storage, their uses, and their limits. Self-Healing Polymers

JAH-PROMAT-000007 · self-healing polymers · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Programmable Materials Database, catalog item 15 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Self-Healing Polymers. A self-healing polymer is a plastic that repairs its own damage, closing a crack or a cut without outside help. Two designs exist. One carries tiny capsules of liquid glue inside the plastic, and when a crack breaks a capsule the glue flows out, fills the crack, and hardens. The other is built from reversible bonds, which are chemical links that can break and reform, so warming the plastic lets the bonds reconnect across the crack. Self-healing polymers are studied for phone coatings, car paint, aircraft parts, and artificial skin, because a surface that heals itself lasts longer and needs less replacement. The research questions are how many times one spot can heal, how strong the healed crack is, and whether healing works in the cold or only when warmed. This record specifies self-healing polymers as documented research: their two designs, their healing, their uses, and their limits. Photo-Responsive Materials

JAH-PROMAT-000008 · photo-responsive materials · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Programmable Materials Database, catalog item 15 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Photo-Responsive Materials. A photo-responsive material is a material that changes when light falls on it, and it is the most convenient programmable material, because light needs no wires and no contact. The changes include colour, as in photochromic glass that darkens in sunlight, shape, as in polymers that bend toward light, and stiffness, as in gels that harden under ultraviolet light. The cause is usually a molecule that flips between two shapes when it absorbs light, and flips back when the light is gone or when a different colour arrives. Researchers use photo-responsive materials for windows that shade themselves, for 3D printing where light hardens one layer at a time, and for soft robots steered by a lamp instead of a cable. The questions are how fast the flip is, how many flips the material survives, and how to make the response work in ordinary daylight rather than only under strong lamps. This record specifies photo-responsive materials as documented research: their flips, their changes, their uses, and their limits. 4D Printing

JAH-PROMAT-000009 · 4d printing · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Programmable Materials Database, catalog item 15 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is 4D Printing. 4D printing is printing objects that change shape over time when triggered, so the fourth dimension is time itself. The printer builds an object from shape-memory polymers or hydrogels, which are water-filled gels, arranged so that different parts respond differently to the same trigger. When the object is heated, wetted, or lit, it folds, curls, or expands into its final shape, which means a flat sheet can become a box, a tube, or a flower without any assembly. Researchers use 4D printing for packaging that folds itself, for medical models that expand after insertion, as research with no medical advice given here, and for space parts that unfold after launch. The questions are how to design the flat shape so it folds correctly, how precise the final shape is, and how many times the object can fold and unfold. This record specifies 4D printing as documented research: its materials, its folding, its uses, and its limits. The future research type covered here is Programmable Matter and Claytronics. Programmable matter is matter whose properties can be changed by a signal, and claytronics is the research proposal that matter could be made of millions of tiny robots, called catoms, that stick together, move, and rearrange themselves into any shape on command. A claytronics object would be a swarm of catoms, each one millimetre across or smaller, each able to talk to its neighbours, hold on, and let go, so a phone could become a cup, a chair could become a table, and a broken part could rebuild itself from its neighbours. No such matter exists: the proposal is theoretical research, maturity E4 (Experimental) at best for its parts, with working demonstrations limited to small swarms of larger robots. This record specifies programmable matter and claytronics as research concepts: the catom, the swarm rules, the shape-changing goal, and the honest statement that the full vision is not built, with the parts that do exist documented separately. Metamaterials with Tunable Properties

JAH-PROMAT-000011 · metamaterials with tunable properties · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Programmable Materials Database, catalog item 15 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Metamaterials with Tunable Properties. A metamaterial is a material whose properties come from its structure rather than its chemistry, and a tunable metamaterial is one whose structure can be changed on command, so its properties change with it. The structure is usually a repeating pattern of tiny shapes, such as rings, rods, or holes, smaller than the wavelength of the light or sound it acts on. By stretching the pattern, heating it, or applying a voltage, researchers shift what the material does: it can bend light one way, then another, or block one sound frequency, then a different one. Tunable metamaterials are studied for lenses that change focus, for antennas that retune themselves, and for cloaks that hide objects from one kind of wave. The questions are how far the tuning can reach, how fast it can switch, and how to build the tiny patterns cheaply at large sizes. This record specifies tunable metamaterials as documented research: their patterns, their tuning, their uses, and their limits. The future research type covered here is Stimuli-Responsive Hydrogels. A hydrogel is a water-filled gel that can swell, shrink, and carry other materials, and a stimuli-responsive hydrogel is one that does so on command when a signal arrives. The gel is a network of polymer chains holding water, and when the temperature, acidity, or salt level changes, the network tightens or loosens, squeezing water out or drinking it in, so the gel can swell to many times its dry size or shrink to a fraction. Researchers use responsive hydrogels for soft robot muscles that swell to grip, for valves that open and close with temperature, and for laboratory models that release their contents on a signal, always as research models with no medical advice and no dosing information given here. The questions are how fast the swelling is, how strong the swollen gel is, and how many swell cycles it survives. This record specifies stimuli-responsive hydrogels as documented research: their network, their swelling, their uses, and their limits. Piezoelectric Materials

JAH-PROMAT-000013 · piezoelectric materials · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Programmable Materials Database, catalog item 15 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Piezoelectric Materials. A piezoelectric material is one that produces an electric charge when it is squeezed, and moves when a charge is applied, which makes it a two-way bridge between force and electricity. The cause is a crystal structure with no centre of symmetry, so squeezing it shifts positive and negative charges apart and a voltage appears, and applying a voltage shifts the charges back and the crystal moves. Piezoelectric materials are used in lighters that spark when clicked, in ultrasound machines that send and receive sound through the body, as research instruments with no medical advice given here, in precise motors that move in steps smaller than a hair, and in energy harvesters that collect power from footsteps and vibration. The questions are how much charge a squeeze produces, how far the material moves under voltage, and how to make large pieces without cracks. This record specifies piezoelectric materials as documented research: their crystal cause, their two directions, their uses, and their limits. Thermoelectric Materials

JAH-PROMAT-000014 · thermoelectric materials · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Programmable Materials Database, catalog item 15 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Thermoelectric Materials. A thermoelectric material is one that converts heat directly into electricity, and electricity directly into heating or cooling, with no moving parts. When one side of the material is hot and the other is cold, charge carriers, which are the moving charges inside the material, drift from hot to cold and a voltage appears. Run in reverse, a current through the material pumps heat from one side to the other, so it can cool a spot without a fan. Thermoelectric materials are used to power deep-space probes from the heat of decaying fuel, to cool small electronics, and to harvest waste heat from engines and factories. The research question is efficiency: the best materials convert only a small fraction of the heat, so the field searches for materials that carry charge well but carry heat badly. This record specifies thermoelectric materials as documented research: their drift, their two directions, their uses, and the efficiency limit. Chromic Materials

JAH-PROMAT-000015 · chromic materials · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Programmable Materials Database, catalog item 15 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Chromic Materials. A chromic material is one that changes colour under a signal, and the family is named by its trigger: photochromic for light, thermochromic for heat, and electrochromic for electricity. Photochromic glass darkens in sunlight and clears indoors, thermochromic paint changes colour with temperature so a mug or a wall can show how hot it is, and electrochromic windows tint when a small voltage is applied and clear when it is removed. The cause is usually a molecule that flips shape or a crystal that changes how it absorbs light, and the flip is reversible, so the colour can switch back and forth many times. Researchers use chromic materials for windows that shade themselves, for displays that need no backlight, and for sensors that show temperature or voltage as colour. The questions are how fast the colour changes, how many switches the material survives, and how vivid the colours can be. This record specifies chromic materials as documented research: their three triggers, their flips, their uses, and their limits. The future research type covered here is Programmable Stiffness Materials. A programmable stiffness material is one whose stiffness, which is how much it resists bending, can be changed on command, so one object can be soft when it must adapt and stiff when it must hold. Three designs exist. One uses layers that slide freely until a vacuum pulls them tight, like a bag of coffee grounds that turns solid when the air is sucked out. One uses a fluid that thickens under a magnetic field, as in magnetorheological fluids. One uses a polymer that softens when warmed and stiffens when cooled. The uses include robot grippers that go soft to wrap an object and stiff to lift it, prosthetic sockets that adapt to the wearer, as research with no medical advice given here, and protective gear that stays flexible until impact makes it stiff. The questions are how fast the switch is, how many times stiffer the stiff state is, and how the material behaves after thousands of switches. This record specifies programmable stiffness materials as documented research: their three designs, their switch, their uses, and their limits. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Soft Robotics Materials. Soft robotics materials are the soft, flexible materials that soft robots are built from: silicone rubbers, hydrogels, which are water-filled gels, electroactive polymers, and shape-memory polymers, chosen so the robot can bend, stretch, and squeeze without breaking. A soft robot moves by inflating chambers, by swelling gels, or by applying voltage to a polymer, and it can wrap around a fragile object, crawl through a narrow gap, or work beside a person without the danger of a hard metal arm. The materials must be soft enough to be safe, strong enough to do work, and durable enough to survive thousands of bends. Researchers use soft robotics materials for grippers that handle fruit, for wearable helpers that move with the body, and for exploration robots that squeeze into ruins, as engineering research. The questions are how much force a soft robot can produce, how precisely it can be controlled, and how to sense what it is touching. JAH-PROMAT-000019 · self-assembling materials · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Programmable Materials Database, catalog item 15 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Self-Assembling Materials. Self-assembly is the joining of parts into larger structures without outside guidance, and self-assembling materials are designed so their parts find each other and lock together on their own. The parts carry the instructions in their shape: a block with a bump fits a block with a hole, a molecule with a sticky end finds its partner, and a swarm of small pieces settles into a sheet, a tube, or a lattice. The forces that drive assembly are the ordinary forces of chemistry and physics: attraction between opposite charges, the pull of water-fearing surfaces, and the lock of matching shapes. Researchers use self-assembly for coatings that build themselves one layer at a time, for capsules that form around a cargo, and for structures too small to build by hand. The questions are how to design parts that assemble into exactly one structure, how to fix mistakes, and how to take the structure apart again. This record specifies self-assembling materials as documented research: their parts, their forces, their uses, and their limits. Programmable Materials Testing and Standards

JAH-PROMAT-000020 · programmable materials testing · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Programmable Materials Database, catalog item 15 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Programmable Materials Testing and Standards. Testing and standards are the shared rules that say whether a programmable material does what it claims, and they are what turn laboratory demonstrations into trustworthy engineering. The tests measure the four questions of the field: the trigger level that starts the change, the size and speed of the response, the reversibility, which is whether the material switches back fully, and the endurance, which is how many switches it survives. A standard test states its conditions exactly, the temperature, the voltage, the load, and the number of cycles, so that a result in one laboratory can be compared with a result in another. Without standards, every maker reports its own numbers under its own conditions, and buyers cannot compare materials at all. This record specifies testing and standards for programmable materials: the four measures, the test conditions that must be stated, and the rule that a claim without a stated test is not a claim. The system identity is fixed: this is a future programmable materials record, built as one database pair with a public edition and a Signature edition, each holding 24 records, so the pair holds 48 records in total. Programmable Materials in Medicine

JAH-PROMAT-000021 · programmable materials in medicine · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Programmable Materials Database, catalog item 15 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Programmable Materials in Medicine. Programmable materials in medicine is the research use of responsive materials in medical devices and models, and this record states its boundary first: it is research only, with no medical advice and no dosing information given here, and no cure claims are made. The research uses shape-memory alloys in stents that unfold inside the body, hydrogels that release their contents on a signal in laboratory models, and textiles that sense stretch and pressure for monitoring, all as documented research and engineering. The materials must pass far stricter tests than ordinary materials: they must not harm the body, they must work reliably for years, and they must fail safely if they fail at all. The questions are how to prove long-term safety, how to test a material that changes shape inside the body, and how to regulate a device whose properties change on command. This record specifies the medical research uses, the safety tests, and the boundary that keeps the work as research, never as advice. Programmable Materials in Buildings

JAH-PROMAT-000022 · programmable materials in buildings · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Programmable Materials Database, catalog item 15 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Programmable Materials in Buildings. Programmable materials in buildings are responsive materials used as walls, windows, and roofs that adapt to weather and to the people inside. Phase-change walls store daytime heat and release it at night, electrochromic windows tint in bright sun and clear in cloud, shape-memory vents open when the room warms and close when it cools, and self-healing coatings repair small cracks before water gets in. The promise is a building that uses less energy because its skin does part of the heating, cooling, and shading work itself. The questions are cost, because responsive materials are dearer than ordinary ones, endurance, because a building must last decades, and control, because a wall that changes on its own must never trap people in the dark or the cold. This record specifies programmable materials in buildings as documented research: their four uses, their energy savings, and their limits. The system identity is fixed: this is a future programmable materials record, built as one database pair with a public edition and a Signature edition, each holding 24 records, so the pair holds 48 records in total. Programmable Materials and Sustainability

JAH-PROMAT-000023 · programmable materials and sustainability · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Programmable Materials Database, catalog item 15 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Programmable Materials and Sustainability. Sustainability is the question of whether a material can be made, used, and disposed of without doing more harm than good, and programmable materials must answer it like any other material. On the good side, a self-healing coating makes a product last longer, a phase-change wall saves energy for decades, and a 4D-printed part ships flat and folds itself, saving transport. On the hard side, responsive materials often need rare metals, complex chemistry, and energy-hungry manufacturing, and a material that is hard to take apart is hard to recycle. The research questions are how to make responsive materials from common, safe ingredients, how to design them for repair and recycling, and how to measure their whole life, from mining to disposal, honestly. This record specifies sustainability for programmable materials: the savings, the costs, and the whole-life measure that weighs them. The system identity is fixed: this is a future programmable materials record, built as one database pair with a public edition and a Signature edition, each holding 24 records, so the pair holds 48 records in total. The maturity label for this record is E4, which stands for Experimental, meaning experiments exist but the technology is not yet routine. A maturity label is a short code saying how mature a technology is, and E4 marks this work as research, never as a finished or proven system. The future research type covered here is The Future of Programmable Materials. The future of programmable materials research is the set of open directions the field is moving toward, stated here as research directions with maturity label E4 (Experimental), never as predictions or guaranteed breakthroughs. The directions are: materials that respond to several signals at once, materials that learn from past switches, self-healing materials that heal many times at full strength, programmable matter that rearranges at larger scales, and materials designed from the start for repair and recycling. Each direction carries the same rules as the rest of this pair: claims tied to stated tests, designs called designs, research only in medical uses with no medical advice given, and no claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence anywhere. The planning windows in the catalog run from 2026 to 2035 for the nearest work and further for the rest, and those windows are planning windows, not promises. This record specifies the future directions, their current status, and the rules that will govern them. 4. History

What Programmable Materials Are

JAH-PROMAT-000001 · programmable materials identity · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

5. How It Works — Construction Workflow

What Programmable Materials Are

JAH-PROMAT-000001 · programmable materials identity · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Problem: The research question this record answers is the gap between having a named future capability and having a working research system for it. A name alone builds nothing: without a specification, a database structure, search and retrieval, working source code, a reusable generator, a website interface, documentation, a demonstration, automated tests, and validation results, the capability exists only as words. Without a shared identity, every reactive material is called programmable, and the word loses the meaning that makes the field worth studying. The wider problem is duplication and drift: builders who cannot find what already exists rebuild it badly, contradict it, or miss it entirely, and systems that measure and identify things in different ways drift apart until their records no longer agree. This specification answers both problems by fixing one identity, one structure, one retrieval rule, and one validation ladder for the whole type, so that every later build of this type can be checked against the same standard.

Method: Complete specification. The research specification must satisfy every one of these requirements: the pair holds the long full official specification for programmable materials research under the section-8 research standard; every record carries maturity label E4 (Experimental) with a plain-words definition; every public record cites at least two sources fetched and checked on 2026-11-17, with web address, resource identifier, and retrieval date; every record carries a working Python demo with expected output captured by execution, and a working program; every record carries a JCC code, which stands for JAH Condensed Code, that round-trips; the first-choice identifier prefix JAH-PMAT- is taken by the live pattern-matcher-all-types database, so this pair uses JAH-PROMAT- instead, verified free in the live manifest; no claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established anywhere in the pair; the pair is package-only: built and checked in the workspace, not pushed, not live, and not merged. Research standard. Every future research project in this specification carries ten parts, from section 8 of the catalog: the research question, which is the single question the project sets out to answer; existing knowledge and source references, which is what is already known and where it is documented; the proposed innovation, which is the new idea this project adds; the technical specification, which is the complete detailed description of what is built; the mathematical and scientific foundations, which are the equations and established science the work rests on; the prototype or simulation plan, which is how a working model or computer model is built and run; the testing and validation criteria, which are the checks that decide whether the work passes; the risks and limitations, which are what can go wrong and what the work cannot do; the evidence and development status, which is what has actually been shown and how mature the work is; and the future expansion opportunities, which are the next steps the work opens up. This record covers all ten parts: the research question is in the problem, the existing knowledge is in the description and sources, the proposed innovation is in the method, the technical specification is the method in full, the foundations are in the math proof and science proof, the prototype or simulation plan is in the demo and program, the testing and validation criteria are in the results, the risks and limitations are in the limits, the evidence and development status are in the answer and the maturity label, and the future expansion opportunities are in the applications and the expansion instructions. The system is built from these parts, each of which is specified in full: Trigger, which is the signal, such as heat, light, electricity, or a magnetic field; Response, which is the change the material makes; Reversibility, which is whether the material can switch back to its first state; Endurance, which is how many switches the material survives. Database structure. The database holds records of kind future programmable materials record. Every record carries an identifier, a title, a subject, a description, a history, a problem, a method, reported results, a science proof, a math proof, a formal equation with a plain-words gloss, a scientific equation with a proof sketch, a skeptical analysis, an applications section, a verification path, a specification block with dimensions and care notes, key figures, a teaching-ladder rank, a dataset note, an evidence label, a maturity label, a demo with expected output, a working program, sources with web addresses and retrieval dates, network links, a generator block, and three scales. The checkable figures for this record are property kinds 3, records per edition 24, pair records 48, sources per record 3, glossary terms 50, sections 16, signals 4. Search and retrieval. Retrieval works by exact identifier, by title, by subject, and by full-text search over every prose field, and every retrieved record returns with its sources and its provenance attached, so a retrieved fact can always be traced back to the page it came from. Working source code. The demo and the program in this record are real working Python code, not a description of code: the demo recomputes the record's own figures twice and compares SHA-256 digests, which are 256-bit fingerprints computed with the standard hashlib library, and the program wraps the same computation in a class with a run method, so the demo and the program agree by construction. Reusable generator. A seeded boundless generator, which is a program that creates new records on demand from a starting number called a seed, serves this database, and it must pass its generator gates, which are checks a generator must pass before its output is accepted: the emitted record kind must match the manifest record kind, the slug must use the single signature form, the identifier must use the database's own prefix, and the hard stop must be 1,000,000,000. Website interface. The website interface, which is the set of web pages through which a person or a program uses the system, gives one page per record with the full record, a search page, and an accessible version that is plain and readable without JavaScript, which is the programming language that runs in the browser. Documentation. The documentation explains the system in plain words first, defines every term it uses, and puts sources and citations last, so a reader meets the meaning before the references. Demonstration. The demo in this record is the demonstration: it runs, it prints its results, and its output is captured as the expected output, never written by hand. Automated tests. The automated tests, which are a test suite, a collection of automated tests, check the demo, the program, the generator, the record kind, the slug form, the identifier prefix, the hard stop, and the JCC code, which stands for JAH Condensed Code, a short code that points to one exact record. Expansion instructions. To expand this database, add records in the same shape, keep the identifiers sequential, keep the sources fetched and dated, and never present generated content as authentic public record.

Results reported: Validation results. This record is validated by execution, not by assertion: its demo was executed at build time and its printed output was captured as the expected output, its program was executed and returned a deterministic match, its figures were recomputed twice with matching SHA-256 digests, and the whole Wave 1 Group A set passed CHECK 2, which is the second validation pass that re-checks every record, every demo, every program, every JCC code round-trip, every source web address, and every generator gate by execution. The acceptance tests for this future research type are: the record is complete, with every required field filled; the demo executes and its output matches the expected output exactly; the program executes and reports a deterministic match; the JCC code round-trips, which means recomputing it from the record gives the same code; every cited source returns HTTP 200, which is the web server's answer that the page was found, when fetched; the generator passes its gates; the maturity label is present and defined in plain words; and the record defines its terms in plain words. A record that fails any acceptance test is not filed as validated. The demo figures for this record are property kinds 3, records per edition 24, pair records 48, sources per record 3, glossary terms 50, sections 16, signals 4, and the demo prints them twice so that any change in the figures changes the printed output and the digests with it.

When it applies: This record applies whenever the Master AI (artificial intelligence) needs to build, find, or check a future programmable materials record: it gives the identity, the complete specification, the database structure, the search and retrieval rules, the working code, the generator, the website interface, the documentation shape, the demonstration, the automated tests, the validation results, the version history, the source provenance, the maturity label, and the expansion instructions in one place. JAH-PROMAT-000002 · shape-memory alloys · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Method: Complete specification. The research specification must satisfy every one of these requirements: the pair holds the long full official specification for programmable materials research under the section-8 research standard; every record carries maturity label E4 (Experimental) with a plain-words definition; every public record cites at least two sources fetched and checked on 2026-11-17, with web address, resource identifier, and retrieval date; every record carries a working Python demo with expected output captured by execution, and a working program; every record carries a JCC code, which stands for JAH Condensed Code, that round-trips; the first-choice identifier prefix JAH-PMAT- is taken by the live pattern-matcher-all-types database, so this pair uses JAH-PROMAT- instead, verified free in the live manifest; no claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established anywhere in the pair; the pair is package-only: built and checked in the workspace, not pushed, not live, and not merged. Research standard. Every future research project in this specification carries ten parts, from section 8 of the catalog: the research question, which is the single question the project sets out to answer; existing knowledge and source references, which is what is already known and where it is documented; the proposed innovation, which is the new idea this project adds; the technical specification, which is the complete detailed description of what is built; the mathematical and scientific foundations, which are the equations and established science the work rests on; the prototype or simulation plan, which is how a working model or computer model is built and run; the testing and validation criteria, which are the checks that decide whether the work passes; the risks and limitations, which are what can go wrong and what the work cannot do; the evidence and development status, which is what has actually been shown and how mature the work is; and the future expansion opportunities, which are the next steps the work opens up. This record covers all ten parts: the research question is in the problem, the existing knowledge is in the description and sources, the proposed innovation is in the method, the technical specification is the method in full, the foundations are in the math proof and science proof, the prototype or simulation plan is in the demo and program, the testing and validation criteria are in the results, the risks and limitations are in the limits, the evidence and development status are in the answer and the maturity label, and the future expansion opportunities are in the applications and the expansion instructions. The system is built from these parts, each of which is specified in full: Nitinol, which is the nickel-titanium alloy that best shows shape memory; Training, which is holding the alloy in a shape at high temperature so it remembers it; Crystal structure, which is the arrangement of atoms inside the metal; Actuator, which is a part that moves something. Database structure. The database holds records of kind future programmable materials record. Every record carries an identifier, a title, a subject, a description, a history, a problem, a method, reported results, a science proof, a math proof, a formal equation with a plain-words gloss, a scientific equation with a proof sketch, a skeptical analysis, an applications section, a verification path, a specification block with dimensions and care notes, key figures, a teaching-ladder rank, a dataset note, an evidence label, a maturity label, a demo with expected output, a working program, sources with web addresses and retrieval dates, network links, a generator block, and three scales. The checkable figures for this record are metals in alloy 2, records per edition 24, pair records 48, sources per record 3, glossary terms 50, sections 16, crystal states 2. Search and retrieval. Retrieval works by exact identifier, by title, by subject, and by full-text search over every prose field, and every retrieved record returns with its sources and its provenance attached, so a retrieved fact can always be traced back to the page it came from. Working source code. The demo and the program in this record are real working Python code, not a description of code: the demo recomputes the record's own figures twice and compares SHA-256 digests, which are 256-bit fingerprints computed with the standard hashlib library, and the program wraps the same computation in a class with a run method, so the demo and the program agree by construction. Reusable generator. A seeded boundless generator, which is a program that creates new records on demand from a starting number called a seed, serves this database, and it must pass its generator gates, which are checks a generator must pass before its output is accepted: the emitted record kind must match the manifest record kind, the slug must use the single signature form, the identifier must use the database's own prefix, and the hard stop must be 1,000,000,000. Website interface. The website interface, which is the set of web pages through which a person or a program uses the system, gives one page per record with the full record, a search page, and an accessible version that is plain and readable without JavaScript, which is the programming language that runs in the browser. Documentation. The documentation explains the system in plain words first, defines every term it uses, and puts sources and citations last, so a reader meets the meaning before the references. Demonstration. The demo in this record is the demonstration: it runs, it prints its results, and its output is captured as the expected output, never written by hand. Automated tests. The automated tests, which are a test suite, a collection of automated tests, check the demo, the program, the generator, the record kind, the slug form, the identifier prefix, the hard stop, and the JCC code, which stands for JAH Condensed Code, a short code that points to one exact record. Expansion instructions. To expand this database, add records in the same shape, keep the identifiers sequential, keep the sources fetched and dated, and never present generated content as authentic public record.

Results reported: Validation results. This record is validated by execution, not by assertion: its demo was executed at build time and its printed output was captured as the expected output, its program was executed and returned a deterministic match, its figures were recomputed twice with matching SHA-256 digests, and the whole Wave 1 Group A set passed CHECK 2, which is the second validation pass that re-checks every record, every demo, every program, every JCC code round-trip, every source web address, and every generator gate by execution. The acceptance tests for this future research type are: the record is complete, with every required field filled; the demo executes and its output matches the expected output exactly; the program executes and reports a deterministic match; the JCC code round-trips, which means recomputing it from the record gives the same code; every cited source returns HTTP 200, which is the web server's answer that the page was found, when fetched; the generator passes its gates; the maturity label is present and defined in plain words; and the record defines its terms in plain words. The checkable figures for this record are switch time ms 1, records per edition 24, pair records 48, sources per record 3, glossary terms 50, sections 16, uses 3. Search and retrieval. Retrieval works by exact identifier, by title, by subject, and by full-text search over every prose field, and every retrieved record returns with its sources and its provenance attached, so a retrieved fact can always be traced back to the page it came from. Working source code. The demo and the program in this record are real working Python code, not a description of code: the demo recomputes the record's own figures twice and compares SHA-256 digests, which are 256-bit fingerprints computed with the standard hashlib library, and the program wraps the same computation in a class with a run method, so the demo and the program agree by construction. Reusable generator. A seeded boundless generator, which is a program that creates new records on demand from a starting number called a seed, serves this database, and it must pass its generator gates, which are checks a generator must pass before its output is accepted: the emitted record kind must match the manifest record kind, the slug must use the single signature form, the identifier must use the database's own prefix, and the hard stop must be 1,000,000,000. Website interface. The website interface, which is the set of web pages through which a person or a program uses the system, gives one page per record with the full record, a search page, and an accessible version that is plain and readable without JavaScript, which is the programming language that runs in the browser. Documentation. The documentation explains the system in plain words first, defines every term it uses, and puts sources and citations last, so a reader meets the meaning before the references. Demonstration. The demo in this record is the demonstration: it runs, it prints its results, and its output is captured as the expected output, never written by hand. Automated tests. The automated tests, which are a test suite, a collection of automated tests, check the demo, the program, the generator, the record kind, the slug form, the identifier prefix, the hard stop, and the JCC code, which stands for JAH Condensed Code, a short code that points to one exact record. Expansion instructions. To expand this database, add records in the same shape, keep the identifiers sequential, keep the sources fetched and dated, and never present generated content as authentic public record.

Results reported: Validation results. This record is validated by execution, not by assertion: its demo was executed at build time and its printed output was captured as the expected output, its program was executed and returned a deterministic match, its figures were recomputed twice with matching SHA-256 digests, and the whole Wave 1 Group A set passed CHECK 2, which is the second validation pass that re-checks every record, every demo, every program, every JCC code round-trip, every source web address, and every generator gate by execution. The acceptance tests for this future research type are: the record is complete, with every required field filled; the demo executes and its output matches the expected output exactly; the program executes and reports a deterministic match; the JCC code round-trips, which means recomputing it from the record gives the same code; every cited source returns HTTP 200, which is the web server's answer that the page was found, when fetched; the generator passes its gates; the maturity label is present and defined in plain words; and the record defines its terms in plain words. A record that fails any acceptance test is not filed as validated. The demo figures for this record are switch time ms 1, records per edition 24, pair records 48, sources per record 3, glossary terms 50, sections 16, uses 3, and the demo prints them twice so that any change in the figures changes the printed output and the digests with it.

Phase-Change Materials

JAH-PROMAT-000006 · phase-change materials · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Problem: The research question this record answers is the gap between having a named future capability and having a working research system for it. A name alone builds nothing: without a specification, a database structure, search and retrieval, working source code, a reusable generator, a website interface, documentation, a demonstration, automated tests, and validation results, the capability exists only as words. Without phase-change materials, a building must burn energy to stay cool, where a melting wax could hold the heat for free. The wider problem is duplication and drift: builders who cannot find what already exists rebuild it badly, contradict it, or miss it entirely, and systems that measure and identify things in different ways drift apart until their records no longer agree. This specification answers both problems by fixing one identity, one structure, one retrieval rule, and one validation ladder for the whole type, so that every later build of this type can be checked against the same standard.

A name alone builds nothing: without a specification, a database structure, search and retrieval, working source code, a reusable generator, a website interface, documentation, a demonstration, automated tests, and validation results, the capability exists only as words. Without 4D printing, every folded object must be assembled by hand or by machine, where a printed sheet could fold itself. The wider problem is duplication and drift: builders who cannot find what already exists rebuild it badly, contradict it, or miss it entirely, and systems that measure and identify things in different ways drift apart until their records no longer agree. This specification answers both problems by fixing one identity, one structure, one retrieval rule, and one validation ladder for the whole type, so that every later build of this type can be checked against the same standard.

Method: Complete specification. The research specification must satisfy every one of these requirements: the pair holds the long full official specification for programmable materials research under the section-8 research standard; every record carries maturity label E4 (Experimental) with a plain-words definition; every public record cites at least two sources fetched and checked on 2026-11-17, with web address, resource identifier, and retrieval date; every record carries a working Python demo with expected output captured by execution, and a working program; every record carries a JCC code, which stands for JAH Condensed Code, that round-trips; the first-choice identifier prefix JAH-PMAT- is taken by the live pattern-matcher-all-types database, so this pair uses JAH-PROMAT- instead, verified free in the live manifest; no claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established anywhere in the pair; the pair is package-only: built and checked in the workspace, not pushed, not live, and not merged. Research standard. Every future research project in this specification carries ten parts, from section 8 of the catalog: the research question, which is the single question the project sets out to answer; existing knowledge and source references, which is what is already known and where it is documented; the proposed innovation, which is the new idea this project adds; the technical specification, which is the complete detailed description of what is built; the mathematical and scientific foundations, which are the equations and established science the work rests on; the prototype or simulation plan, which is how a working model or computer model is built and run; the testing and validation criteria, which are the checks that decide whether the work passes; the risks and limitations, which are what can go wrong and what the work cannot do; the evidence and development status, which is what has actually been shown and how mature the work is; and the future expansion opportunities, which are the next steps the work opens up. This record covers all ten parts: the research question is in the problem, the existing knowledge is in the description and sources, the proposed innovation is in the method, the technical specification is the method in full, the foundations are in the math proof and science proof, the prototype or simulation plan is in the demo and program, the testing and validation criteria are in the results, the risks and limitations are in the limits, the evidence and development status are in the answer and the maturity label, and the future expansion opportunities are in the applications and the expansion instructions. The system is built from these parts, each of which is specified in full: Fourth dimension, which is time: the change of shape after printing; Hydrogel, which is a water-filled gel that can swell, shrink, and carry other materials; Fold, which is the bending of a printed part into its final shape; Assembly, which is the putting together of separate parts. Database structure. The database holds records of kind future programmable materials record. Every record carries an identifier, a title, a subject, a description, a history, a problem, a method, reported results, a science proof, a math proof, a formal equation with a plain-words gloss, a scientific equation with a proof sketch, a skeptical analysis, an applications section, a verification path, a specification block with dimensions and care notes, key figures, a teaching-ladder rank, a dataset note, an evidence label, a maturity label, a demo with expected output, a working program, sources with web addresses and retrieval dates, network links, a generator block, and three scales. The checkable figures for this record are printed shapes 3, records per edition 24, pair records 48, sources per record 3, glossary terms 50, sections 16, triggers 3. Search and retrieval. Retrieval works by exact identifier, by title, by subject, and by full-text search over every prose field, and every retrieved record returns with its sources and its provenance attached, so a retrieved fact can always be traced back to the page it came from. Working source code. The demo and the program in this record are real working Python code, not a description of code: the demo recomputes the record's own figures twice and compares SHA-256 digests, which are 256-bit fingerprints computed with the standard hashlib library, and the program wraps the same computation in a class with a run method, so the demo and the program agree by construction. Reusable generator. A seeded boundless generator, which is a program that creates new records on demand from a starting number called a seed, serves this database, and it must pass its generator gates, which are checks a generator must pass before its output is accepted: the emitted record kind must match the manifest record kind, the slug must use the single signature form, the identifier must use the database's own prefix, and the hard stop must be 1,000,000,000. Website interface. The website interface, which is the set of web pages through which a person or a program uses the system, gives one page per record with the full record, a search page, and an accessible version that is plain and readable without JavaScript, which is the programming language that runs in the browser. Documentation. The documentation explains the system in plain words first, defines every term it uses, and puts sources and citations last, so a reader meets the meaning before the references. Demonstration. The demo in this record is the demonstration: it runs, it prints its results, and its output is captured as the expected output, never written by hand. Automated tests. The automated tests, which are a test suite, a collection of automated tests, check the demo, the program, the generator, the record kind, the slug form, the identifier prefix, the hard stop, and the JCC code, which stands for JAH Condensed Code, a short code that points to one exact record. Expansion instructions. To expand this database, add records in the same shape, keep the identifiers sequential, keep the sources fetched and dated, and never present generated content as authentic public record.

Results reported: Validation results. This record is validated by execution, not by assertion: its demo was executed at build time and its printed output was captured as the expected output, its program was executed and returned a deterministic match, its figures were recomputed twice with matching SHA-256 digests, and the whole Wave 1 Group A set passed CHECK 2, which is the second validation pass that re-checks every record, every demo, every program, every JCC code round-trip, every source web address, and every generator gate by execution. The acceptance tests for this future research type are: the record is complete, with every required field filled; the demo executes and its output matches the expected output exactly; the program executes and reports a deterministic match; the JCC code round-trips, which means recomputing it from the record gives the same code; every cited source returns HTTP 200, which is the web server's answer that the page was found, when fetched; the generator passes its gates; the maturity label is present and defined in plain words; and the record defines its terms in plain words. A record that fails any acceptance test is not filed as validated. The demo figures for this record are printed shapes 3, records per edition 24, pair records 48, sources per record 3, glossary terms 50, sections 16, triggers 3, and the demo prints them twice so that any change in the figures changes the printed output and the digests with it.

When it applies: This record applies whenever the Master AI (artificial intelligence) needs to build, find, or check a future programmable materials record: it gives the identity, the complete specification, the database structure, the search and retrieval rules, the working code, the generator, the website interface, the documentation shape, the demonstration, the automated tests, the validation results, the version history, the source provenance, the maturity label, and the expansion instructions in one place. The connection rule from the catalog is that one type feeds the next: a database can supply a website, the website can use a generator, and the generator can be registered in the generator database, so the Master AI can discover and reuse all three in future projects. Future expansion opportunities for this type are further records in the same shape, deeper coverage of its neighbouring types, and new generators built from its specification.

Piezoelectric Materials

JAH-PROMAT-000013 · piezoelectric materials · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Problem: The research question this record answers is the gap between having a named future capability and having a working research system for it. A name alone builds nothing: without a specification, a database structure, search and retrieval, working source code, a reusable generator, a website interface, documentation, a demonstration, automated tests, and validation results, the capability exists only as words. Without piezoelectric materials, turning a squeeze into a signal needs a separate sensor and a battery, where the crystal itself does both. The wider problem is duplication and drift: builders who cannot find what already exists rebuild it badly, contradict it, or miss it entirely, and systems that measure and identify things in different ways drift apart until their records no longer agree. This specification answers both problems by fixing one identity, one structure, one retrieval rule, and one validation ladder for the whole type, so that every later build of this type can be checked against the same standard.

Method: Complete specification. The research specification must satisfy every one of these requirements: the pair holds the long full official specification for programmable materials research under the section-8 research standard; every record carries maturity label E4 (Experimental) with a plain-words definition; every public record cites at least two sources fetched and checked on 2026-11-17, with web address, resource identifier, and retrieval date; every record carries a working Python demo with expected output captured by execution, and a working program; every record carries a JCC code, which stands for JAH Condensed Code, that round-trips; the first-choice identifier prefix JAH-PMAT- is taken by the live pattern-matcher-all-types database, so this pair uses JAH-PROMAT- instead, verified free in the live manifest; no claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established anywhere in the pair; the pair is package-only: built and checked in the workspace, not pushed, not live, and not merged. Research standard. Every future research project in this specification carries ten parts, from section 8 of the catalog: the research question, which is the single question the project sets out to answer; existing knowledge and source references, which is what is already known and where it is documented; the proposed innovation, which is the new idea this project adds; the technical specification, which is the complete detailed description of what is built; the mathematical and scientific foundations, which are the equations and established science the work rests on; the prototype or simulation plan, which is how a working model or computer model is built and run; the testing and validation criteria, which are the checks that decide whether the work passes; the risks and limitations, which are what can go wrong and what the work cannot do; the evidence and development status, which is what has actually been shown and how mature the work is; and the future expansion opportunities, which are the next steps the work opens up. This record covers all ten parts: the research question is in the problem, the existing knowledge is in the description and sources, the proposed innovation is in the method, the technical specification is the method in full, the foundations are in the math proof and science proof, the prototype or simulation plan is in the demo and program, the testing and validation criteria are in the results, the risks and limitations are in the limits, the evidence and development status are in the answer and the maturity label, and the future expansion opportunities are in the applications and the expansion instructions. The system is built from these parts, each of which is specified in full: Charge, which is the electrical imbalance produced by squeezing; Voltage, which is the electrical push produced by the charge; Crystal, which is the ordered arrangement of atoms in the material; Ultrasound, which is sound too high for the ear to hear, used for imaging and sensing. Working source code. Reusable generator. A seeded boundless generator, which is a program that creates new records on demand from a starting number called a seed, serves this database, and it must pass its generator gates, which are checks a generator must pass before its output is accepted: the emitted record kind must match the manifest record kind, the slug must use the single signature form, the identifier must use the database's own prefix, and the hard stop must be 1,000,000,000.
